class="ltx_contact_name">Affiliation: </span>Nanyang Technological University </span></span></span></span> <span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author"> <span class="ltx_personname">Yi Liu </span><span class="ltx_author_notes"><span class="ltx_author_notes_content"> <span class="ltx_contact ltx_role_affiliation"><span class="ltx_contact_name">Affiliation: </span>Nanyang Technological University </span></span></span></span> <span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author"> <span class="ltx_personname">Dan Williams </span><span class="ltx_author_notes"><span class="ltx_author_notes_content"> <span class="ltx_contact ltx_role_affiliation"><span class="ltx_contact_name">Affiliation: </span>Virginia Tech </span></span></span></span> <span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author"> <span class="ltx_personname">Ying Zhang </span><span class="ltx_author_notes"><span class="ltx_author_notes_content"> <span class="ltx_contact ltx_role_affiliation"><span class="ltx_contact_name">Affiliation: </span>Wake Forest University </span></span></span></span>

</div>

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

The integration of open-source third-party library dependencies in Java development introduces significant security risks when these libraries contain known vulnerabilities. Existing Software Composition Analysis (SCA) tools struggle to effectively detect vulnerable API usage from these libraries due to limitations in understanding API usage semantics and computational challenges in analyzing complex codebases, leading to inaccurate vulnerability alerts that burden development teams and delay critical security fixes.

To address these challenges, we proposed <span id="abstract1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> by leveraging two insights: proof-of-vulnerability test cases demonstrate how vulnerabilities can be triggered in specific contexts, and Large Language Models (LLMs) can understand code semantics. <span id="abstract1.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> combines semantic preprocessing with LLM-powered context analysis for accurate vulnerability detection. <span id="abstract1.2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> first segments source code into meaningful blocks while preserving semantic relationships, then leverages LLM-based reflection to analyze API usage context and determine actual vulnerability impacts. Our evaluation on 55 real-world applications shows that <span id="abstract1.2.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> achieves 83.8% precision, 73.8% recall, 69.0% accuracy, and 78.5% F1-score, outperforming state-of-the-art SCA tools.

</div>

<div class="ltx_pagination ltx_role_newpage">

</div>

<span id="footnotex1" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnotetext"><sup>\*</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>\*</sup><span class="ltx_note_type">footnotetext: </span>Equal contribution.</span></span></span>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Software supply chain attacks through vulnerable open-sourced third-party library APIs pose a growing threat to Java applications \[<a href="#bib.bib69" class="ltx_ref">69</a>, <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a>, <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">3</a>\]. Although these libraries offer various APIs to accelerate functionality implementation, they often contain known vulnerabilities \[<a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a>, <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">48</a>\] (e.g., log4 shell vulnerability \[<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">11</a>\]). These vulnerabilities are publicly documented \[<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">5</a>\], making the information accessible to anyone, including potential attackers. Therefore, when developers integrate these APIs into their projects, they may unintentionally introduce security weaknesses into their applications, creating potential attack vectors for system exploitation \[<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">36</a>, <a href="#bib.bib75" class="ltx_ref">75</a>, <a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>\]. Recent studies have shown that more than 80% of Java applications contain at least one known vulnerability inherited from their third-party dependencies \[<a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a>, <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">48</a>\]. Moreover, attackers can exploit these vulnerabilities through vulnerable APIs, propagating the attack through the software supply chain and further compromising applications built on these dependencies. Such attacks have increased by 1300% \[<a href="#bib.bib62" class="ltx_ref">62</a>\] since 2020.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Existing Software Composition Analysis (SCA) approaches detect vulnerabilities in library dependencies to mitigate Open Source Software (OSS) supply-chain attacks in software applications \[<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">2</a>, <a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">77</a>, <a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">30</a>, <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">18</a>, <a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref">39</a>, <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">4</a>, <a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">6</a>, <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">28</a>, <a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref">54</a>, <a href="#bib.bib74" class="ltx_ref">74</a>\]. For instance, DependencyCheck \[<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">52</a>\], which analyzes dependency configuration files and cross-reference library versions against databases of known vulnerabilities. It flags any found matches based on the library version without considering the API usage in the codebase, which leads to false positives - incorrect identification of vulnerabilities when vulnerable APIs are not used. Consequently, these false positives can erode developers’ trust \[<a href="#bib.bib75" class="ltx_ref">75</a>, <a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">33</a>\] and delay critical security updates when vulnerable APIs are not actively used in the codebase.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

To address the limitations of version-based vulnerability detection, researchers have developed fine-grained approaches \[<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>, <a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>, <a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>, <a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref">54</a>, <a href="#bib.bib74" class="ltx_ref">74</a>, <a href="#bib.bib59" class="ltx_ref">59</a>, <a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">47</a>, <a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">58</a>\] that combine static and dynamic analysis. Eclipse Steady \[<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>\] traces execution paths from potential vulnerability points to application entry points to determine if vulnerable APIs are actually reachable. However, comprehensive data-flow and control-flow analysis of complex codebases face computational barriers \[<a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>, <a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>\], often causing incomplete analysis or excessive processing times. These limitations result in critical vulnerabilities being overlooked in production systems.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

Additionally, assessing the impact of vulnerable APIs from the library extends far beyond theoretical reachability analysis: it requires domain knowledge to understand the API usage semantics within the client application’s context. For instance, when a vulnerable API is susceptible to malformed input, evaluating the actual risk of an API susceptible to malformed input requires expert knowledge to determine if proper defensive mechanisms are implemented downstream. Without considering such code semantics, tools may generate security alerts for non-exploitable scenarios \[<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">44</a>, <a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">45</a>\]. This combination of computational limitations and semantic analysis gap leads to both miss-reporting and over-reporting of vulnerabilities \[<a href="#bib.bib69" class="ltx_ref">69</a>, <a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>\]. This undermines the credibility of vulnerability assessments and places a significant burden on developers, who must manually verify each alert while delaying dependency upgrades due to concerns about breaking changes. Consequently, actual vulnerabilities can persist in the codebase \[<a href="#bib.bib74" class="ltx_ref">74</a>, <a href="#bib.bib71" class="ltx_ref">71</a>\], potentially exposing applications to security risks.

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Our work.</span> To overcome the limitations in the existing approaches, we propose a novel approach <span id="S1.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> leveraging two key insights. First, proof-of-vulnerability (PoV) test cases included in security patches represent a not fully explored yet valuable source that precisely specifies vulnerable API usage patterns. These test cases explicitly demonstrate vulnerable conditions and effectively capture the semantic contexts where vulnerabilities can be exploited. Second, Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated their capabilities in understanding code semantics and programming patterns \[<a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref">67</a>, <a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">46</a>, <a href="#bib.bib78" class="ltx_ref">78</a>, <a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">65</a>\], thereby providing a complementing approach to the program analysis approaches. Building on these insights, <span id="S1.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> leverages LLM’s semantic understanding and patch-provided PoV tests to assess vulnerable API impacts in client applications through two phases: Phase I <span id="S1.p5.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">semantic-preserving preprocessing</span> prepares the application for context-aware analysis by chunking source code into meaningful blocks based on AST structure while preserving metadata like line numbers and file locations. Each block is encoded into vector embeddings and stored with metadata for efficient retrieval, maintaining both semantic and structural relationships. It helps <span id="S1.p5.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> to overcomes the limitations of real-world project sizes and provides an interface for <span id="S1.p5.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> to quickly search the code by semantic. Phase II <span id="S1.p5.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">reflection-based vulnerability detection</span> analyzes API usage by combining PoV test and API information as vectors to locate potential vulnerabilities. It iteratively expands context through reflection-based queries until sufficient information is gathered. The LLM then analyzes this program context with API test functions to determine vulnerability impacts, enabling precise semantic-guided reachability analysis.

</div>

<div id="S1.p6" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S1.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evaluations.</span> We evaluate <span id="S1.p6.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> on a third-party benchmark dataset \[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>\] containing 55 Java projects. We test <span id="S1.p6.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s detection effectiveness at the project level and compare it with two state-of-the-art SCA tools, Eclipse Steady \[<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>\] and VAScanner \[<a href="#bib.bib73" class="ltx_ref">73</a>\]. <span id="S1.p6.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> achieves a precision of 83.8%, recall of 73.8%, accuracy of 69.0%, and F1 score of 78.5%, outperforming the baselines by at least 14%, 182%, 155%, and 103%, respectively, for these metrics. Through extensive ablation studies, we demonstrate that <span id="S1.p6.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> maintains F1 scores between 0.72 and 0.87 across different LLMs and embedding models, with optimal performance achieved at code segment sizes of 2,000-2,500 tokens.

</div>

<div id="S1.p7" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S1.p7.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Contributions.</span> In summary, our paper makes the following contributions as belows:

- <span id="S1.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We develop <span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>- a novel two-phase approach that combines semantic-preserving preprocessing and reflection-based vulnerability detection. It enables efficient and accurate vulnerability localization in real-world projects through semantic-guided reachability analysis powered by LLM.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> supports context-aware analysis of API usage by maintaining both semantic and structural relationships within the Application. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> on a third-party benchmark with 55 real-world applications, our approach outperforms existing approaches across all evaluated metrics.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We extensively evaluated various LLMs and embedding models for vulnerability detection, testing code segments of different maximum length limits. Our experiments revealed that <span id="S1.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Claude-3.5-Sonnet</span> with OpenAI embeddings achieved the highest F1 score.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Threat Model

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our research addresses whether Java applications become vulnerable when using APIs from libraries with known vulnerabilities. When an application uses a vulnerable library API, the application might still be secure if it implements proper security checks. However, determining whether these security checks are adequate requires analyzing the application’s implementation. Modern applications are large and complex, containing millions of lines of code and many library dependencies, making manual security analysis infeasible.

</div>

<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para">

We assume attackers have access to public vulnerability information, including library and application source code, CVEs, and proof-of-concept demonstrations. While current version checkers and static analysis tools only check library versions and code patterns, attackers can analyze how vulnerable library APIs interact with application-level security controls to potentially exploit the library vulnerabilities.

</div>

<div id="S2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Our analysis focuses specifically on vulnerabilities that arise from insufficient or inconsistent security checks around vulnerable library APIs. We assume the application code itself is correctly implemented and not vulnerable to common issues like injection or unsafe deserialization. Our scope is limited to cases where attacker-controlled inputs reach vulnerable library code due to missing or inadequate application checks rather than vulnerabilities in the application’s implementation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Motivation Example

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We illustrate our approach using a real-world vulnerability in Spring Security, a widely-used framework that provides security functionality APIs for Java applications. CVE-2020-5408 \[<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">1</a>\] reveals that in versions 4.2.x prior to 4.2.16, the <span id="S3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">BCryptPasswordEncoder.encode</span> method throws an <span id="S3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">NullPointerException</span> when given a <span id="S3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">null</span> input. Since neither the method’s signature nor its implementation indicates this exception, developers may be unaware of the need to handle null inputs, leading to unexpected application crashes that could be exploited for denial-of-service attacks. This vulnerability was later patched in version 5.4.0-M1 by properly handling null inputs with an <span id="S3.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">IllegalArgumentException</span>.

</div>

<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">Listing <span id="S3.3" class="ltx_ERROR undefined">\thechapter</span>.1 </span>Proof of Vulnerability of Spring Security Library

</div>

Listing <a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref" title="3 Motivation Example ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> demonstrates a case where CVE-2020-5408 (null pointer vulnerability in <span id="S3.p3.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">BCryptPasswordEncoder.encode</span>) does not affect a project despite using the vulnerable API. This example comes from Apache Kylin \[<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">12</a>\], a large-scale analytics project with over 2,000 Java files and 600,000 lines of code (commit “443c2523"). While Kylin uses Spring Security version 4.2.14, which contains this vulnerability, the code remains secure because Kylin’s implementation guarantees that all user passwords are initialized with non-null values before being passed to the encoder, as shown in Listing <a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref" title="3 Motivation Example ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S3.p4" class="ltx_para">

However, existing approaches yield unreliable results when analyzing Kylin’s security. Library version checkers produce false positives by missing defensive code patterns, while static analyzers face scalability issues when tracking complex control and data flows across large codebases.

</div>

<div id="S3.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Our work.</span> To overcome these limitations, we propose <span id="S3.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>, which aims to address two fundamental challenges: (1) efficiently analyzing large codebases without relying on complete call graph construction, and (2) understanding the semantic context of API invocations to determine their true security implications. <span id="S3.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> leverages LLMs for semantic code analysis, enabling accurate vulnerability assessment even in a complex codebase.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Design

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Overview

<figure id="S4.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2506.17798v2/blank_diagram.png" id="S4.F1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:314/104;" width="314" height="104" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F1.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>: </span><span id="S4.F1.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">The Overview of <span id="S4.F1.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

To address the limitations of existing approaches in handling large-scale projects, accurately identifying vulnerable APIs, and understanding the semantic context of code, we propose <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>, a novel system that leverages the power of LLMs to enhance the detection and impact assessment of vulnerable API usages. As shown in Figure <a href="#S4.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 4.1 Overview ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, we design a two-phase process to effectively identify and evaluate the impact of vulnerable API usages within an application. Phase I, Semantic-Preserving Code Preprocessing, addresses the challenges of large codebases and variable naming conventions by transforming the application’s source code into a structured, semantically rich representation. This involves dividing the code into blocks and generating vector embeddings that capture the underlying meaning of each block, regardless of specific identifiers used. These embeddings, along with the original code and metadata, are stored in a database for efficient retrieval. Phase II, Reflection-Based Detection with LLM, tackles the crucial aspect of semantic understanding by leveraging the capabilities of LLMs. Given a vulnerable API and the corresponding PoV test function, <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> uses its vector representation to identify potentially relevant code blocks from the database. It then applies a novel reflection-based querying mechanism, allowing the LLM to iteratively gather contextual information and refine its understanding of how the API is used within the application. This iterative process enables the LLM to determine whether the identified API usage actually leads to a vulnerability in the application. The combination of these two phases allows <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> to perform a comprehensive analysis that goes beyond simple pattern matching, providing a deeper understanding of the code’s behavior and the true impact of potential vulnerabilities.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

The following Sections <a href="#S4.SS2" class="ltx_ref" title="4.2 Phase I: Semantic-preserving Code Preprocessing ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.2</span></a> and <a href="#S4.SS3" class="ltx_ref" title="4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.3</span></a> detail our technical approach and demonstrate how <span id="S4.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> addresses these challenges through its two-phase architecture.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Phase I: Semantic-preserving Code Preprocessing

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this phase, <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> transforms the App’s source code into a format suitable for effective vulnerability analysis. This process involves two key steps: (1) AST-enhanced code segmentation to break down the source code into semantically meaningful blocks, and (2) semantic-preserving code embedding to convert these blocks into dense vector representations. These steps enable <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> to capture and preserve the semantic structure of the code while preparing it for efficient LLM-based analysis.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.2.1 </span>AST-enhanced Code Segmentation

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Code segmentation is crucial for processing large codebases with LLMs due to their limited context window size (typically not more than 200,000 tokens) and the performance degradation observed with longer sequences \[<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">23</a>\]. Therefore, effective code segmentation is essential for maintaining model performance and ensuring comprehensive code analysis. However, fixed-size chunking and line-based splitting can disrupt syntactic units and structural dependencies. By adopting Abstract Syntax Tree (AST)-based segmentation, we preserve the code’s hierarchical structure and semantic relationships, enabling analysis of semantically cohesive blocks rather than arbitrary text segments.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Specifically, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> converts the App’s source code into an AST represented as $G = {\{{g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}}\}}$, where each $g_{i}$ corresponds to a compilation unit in the AST. A naive approach might treat each AST node as an independent block. However, this would lead to an excessive number of small, fragmented blocks, making it difficult to understand the relationships between them. For instance, a single AST node representing a complete if-statement block provides more context than numerous smaller nodes representing individual components of that same if-statement. This fragmentation hinders the ability to capture the overall logic and semantic meaning of the code. Conversely, when nodes are too large (containing multiple methods or classes), they may include substantial irrelevant code that obscures the key vulnerability-related information, potentially causing LLM hallucination. Additionally, oversized nodes limit the number of code segments that can fit within the LLM’s context window, reducing our ability to analyze multiple related code sections simultaneously. To identify semantically meaningful code blocks, we use a heuristic based on node size and inner structure within each compilation unit. <span id="S4.SS2.SSS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> segments the AST from the compilation unit using the following rule:

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

|     |                                                                                                                       |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     | $$g_{i}\rightarrow\left\{ \begin{array}{ll}                                                                           
       {b_{i},} & {{{\text{if~}\hspace{0pt}f_{s\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}z\hspace{0pt}e}\hspace{0pt}{(g_{i})}} < \theta},} \\  
       {{\{{b_{i\hspace{0pt}1},b_{i\hspace{0pt}2},\ldots,b_{i\hspace{0pt}k}}\}},} & {\text{otherwise}.}                       
       \end{array}\phantom{} \right.$$                                                                                        |     |

Here, $\theta$ is a predefined maximum size threshold. For larger nodes (${f_{s\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}z\hspace{0pt}e}\hspace{0pt}{(g_{i})}} \geq \theta$), <span id="S4.SS2.SSS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> performs structural segmentation on the compilation unit node, further dividing it into sub-nodes, specifically focusing on $I\hspace{0pt}m\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}D\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}c\hspace{0pt}l\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}n$, $F\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}l\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}D\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}c\hspace{0pt}l\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}n$,  
${M\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}h\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}D\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}c\hspace{0pt}l\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}n},$ and $C\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}c\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}D\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}c\hspace{0pt}l\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}n$. Intuitively, this approach maintains semantic relationships while creating blocks suitable for LLM-based analysis. The specific thresholds used in this process are further discussed in Section <a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="5.4 RQ3 (Ablation Study) ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.4</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.2.2 </span>Semantic-preserving Code Embedding:

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Following the AST-enhanced code segmentation, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> transforms the resulting code blocks into a format suitable for efficient similarity analysis. Given the set of code blocks $B = {\{{b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{m}}\}}$ obtained from the previous step, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> embeds each block into a dense vector that captures its semantic meaning and functionality. Formally, we define this embedding process as a function $f$:

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

|     |                                                                                                                          |     |                                                                   |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
|     | $${f:{B\rightarrow V}},{\text{where~}{V = {\{{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{m}}\}}}\text{~and~}{v_{i} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{t}}\ }$$ |     | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(1)</span> |

Here, $t$ is the dimensionality of the embedding space. This transformation ensures that semantically similar code blocks are positioned closer together in the $t$-dimensional vector space. To generate these embeddings, we apply pre-trained encoders, such as OpenAI V3 embedding models \[<a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref">50</a>\], that process entire code blocks as input:

|     |                                                                                                                                               |     |                                                                   |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
|     | $${v_{i} = {\text{Encoder}\hspace{0pt}{(b_{i})}}},{{\text{~where~}\hspace{0pt}{\| v_{i}\|}} = {t\hspace{0pt}\text{~for all~}\hspace{0pt}i}}$$ |     | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(2)</span> |

This approach ensures fixed-length embedding vectors regardless of the original block size. We chose this fixed-length block-level embedding over token-level embedding for two primary reasons: 1) <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Comprehensive representation:</span> block-level embedding captures the overall functionality of a code block. For example, consider the following code snippets:  
<span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;"> a) for (int i = 0; i \< n; i++) sum += arr\[i\];  
b) total = Arrays.stream(list).sum();  
</span>Although these snippets have different tokens, they perform semantically similar functionality (i.e., summing elements). Block-level embedding would likely position these blocks closer together in the vector space compared to token-level embedding, which might focus more on the syntactic differences. 2) <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Standardization for consistent comparisons:</span> Fixed-length embeddings provide a uniform format for representing code snippets in a vector space. This standardization enables direct and consistent comparisons between any pair of code embeddings, regardless of the original code’s length or structure. By maintaining a constant dimensionality across all embeddings, this approach avoids additional preprocessing or size adjustments when computing similarities, thus preserving the integrity of the encoded semantic information. Finally, <span id="S4.SS2.SSS2.p2.2.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> stores all processed information in a database $D$, represented as: $D = {\{{d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n}}\}}$, where each entry $d_{i}$ is a tuple containing ${d\hspace{0pt}i} = {(b_{i},v_{i})}$, $b_{i}$ represents the original code block along with its associated metadata (filename, line number range, the original source code snippet, and the AST node type), and $v_{i}$ is its corresponding vector embedding.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.3 </span>Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection

<div id="S4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

This phase focuses on accurately identifying and analyzing vulnerable API usages through a reflection-based approach. The process consists of three primary steps: (1) Candidate Identification, which finds code blocks related to the vulnerable API and the PoV test function; (2) Context-Complete Code Retrieval, which iteratively expands the context around each candidate for a comprehensive understanding of its usage; and (3) Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis, where the LLM, acting as a vulnerability expert, decides if the vulnerability can actually be exploited. This approach addresses a key challenge in vulnerability analysis: the inherent difficulty for static analysis tools to accurately understand code semantics, particularly in complex and dynamic applications. Static analysis primarily relies on pattern matching and struggles to interpret the nuanced meaning and relationships within code. In contrast, our reflection-based method leverages the LLM’s ability to reason about code semantics and context, enabling a more accurate assessment of whether a vulnerable API usage is truly exploitable. This allows <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> to identify vulnerabilities that static analysis might miss, while also reducing false positives.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.3.1 </span>Candidate identification

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Identifying precise API invocation locations is crucial as they serve as starting points for subsequent semantic analysis of vulnerable API usage patterns. <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> begins by leveraging a dual-seed approach: (1) vulnerable APIs and (2) PoV test cases. Vulnerable APIs provide a direct target for identifying potentially unsafe usage, while security test cases offer insights into how these APIs are used in practice, especially in security-sensitive contexts.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Using these seeds, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> employs a two-stage semantic-guided code matching process to accurately pinpoint where the API is actually called within the application code. First, it embeds the code blocks from the project under analysis, the vulnerable API signatures, and the code from security test cases into a shared vector space. Then, for each code block embedding $v_{i}$, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> calculates its cosine similarity to each vulnerable API embedding $v_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i}$ and each security test case embedding $v_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t}$. We select cosine similarity over other metrics, such as Euclidean distance, as it produces equivalent results given that embedding vectors are normalized to a unit length, while the dot product operation in cosine similarity is more efficient \[<a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref">50</a>\].

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

The similarity between a code block embedding $v_{i}$ and the vulnerable API embedding $v_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i}$ is defined as Equation <a href="#S4.E3" class="ltx_ref" title="Equation 3 ‣ 4.3.1 Candidate identification ‣ 4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>:

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                          |     |                                                                   |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
|     | $${{S_{c}\hspace{0pt}{(v_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i},v_{i})}} = \frac{v_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i} \cdot v_{i}}{{\| v_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i}\|}\hspace{0pt}{\| v_{i}\|}}},$$ |     | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(3)</span> |

The similarity between a code block embedding $v_{i}$ and a security test case embedding $v_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t}$ is defined as Equation <a href="#S4.E4" class="ltx_ref" title="Equation 4 ‣ 4.3.1 Candidate identification ‣ 4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>:

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |     |                                                                   |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
|     | $${{S_{c}\hspace{0pt}{(v_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t},v_{i})}} = \frac{v_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t} \cdot v_{i}}{{\| v_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t}\|}\hspace{0pt}{\| v_{i}\|}}},$$ |     | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(4)</span> |

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

To ensure we identify actual API invocation points rather than semantically similar but non-calling code blocks, we implement two filtering stages:

- <span id="S4.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  (F1) <span id="S4.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Embedding Similarity Filter</span> ${S_{c}\hspace{0pt}{(v_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i},v_{i})}} > \tau$ or ${S_{c}\hspace{0pt}{(v_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t},v_{i})}} > \tau$, where $\tau$ is the minimum similarity threshold. This filter considers a code block relevant if it shows strong semantic similarity to either the API’s signature or a security test case. This dual-seed approach broadens the search, increasing the likelihood of capturing relevant code blocks that might be missed by relying solely on API signatures.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  (F2) <span id="S4.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM-based Source Code Verification </span> For code blocks that pass (F1), we apply a second filter (F2) that leverages an LLM grader to verify actual API invocation at the source code level: ${L\hspace{0pt}L\hspace{0pt}M_{g\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}e}\hspace{0pt}{(b_{i},b_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i})}} = \text{"yes"}$. The LLM grader analyzes the code block $b_{i}$ and API signature $b_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i}$ at the source code level to verify actual API invocation, handling cases where semantic similarity alone may not accurately reflect API usage.

  </div>

This two-stage approach combines the efficiency of embedding-based similarity matching with the precision of LLM-based source code verification, ensuring accurate identification of API invocation locations for subsequent vulnerability analysis. By default, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p4.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> considers a code block $c_{i}$ as a candidate for further analysis if:

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                            |     |                                                                   |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
|     | $$\begin{matrix}                                                                                                                                                                                           
       {C{}_{c\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}e} = \{ c{}_{i} = (v{}_{i},b{}_{i},v{}_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i}) \mid c{}_{i} \in D} \\  
       {\land {({{S_{c}\hspace{0pt}{(v_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i},v_{i})}} > {\tau \vee {S_{c}\hspace{0pt}{(v_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t},v_{i})}}} > \tau})}} \\                                
       {\land LLM{}_{g\hspace{0pt}r\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}e}(b{}_{i},b{}_{a\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}i}) = \text{"yes"}\}}                                                                           
       \end{matrix}$$                                                                                                                                                                                              |     | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(5)</span> |

</div>

<figure id="S4.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2506.17798v2/codeflow.png" id="S4.F2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:698/106;" width="698" height="106" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S4.F2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>: </span><span id="S4.F2.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">An example of context-complete code retrieval for BCryptPasswordEncoder.encode() method.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p5" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ 4.3.1 Candidate identification ‣ 4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> illustrates the candidate identification process for projects using BCryptPasswordEncoder, where the POV demonstrates a potential null pointer exception in the <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">encode()</span> method. Starting from the POV, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> retrieves code snippets from its vector database that are semantically related to both the <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">BCryptPasswordEncoder.encode()</span> method and the vulnerability pattern (F1). During the grading and filtering process (F2), <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p5.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> identifies that the second snippet contains the core password encoding implementation with semantic checking (marked with a green checkmark), while filtering out unrelated method definitions (marked with red ’X’s). When the initially retrieved context is insufficient, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS1.p5.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> uses code inference to generate additional queries, ensuring complete context for vulnerability analysis. This procedure effectively combines semantic retrieval with targeted code inference to build a comprehensive code context.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.3.2 </span>Context-complete Code Retrieval

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Accurate vulnerability assessment requires analyzing the complete context of code implementation and API usage patterns. Incomplete context can lead to misinterpretations and inaccurate assessments. To address this, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> employs an LLM-powered iterative reflection technique to achieve comprehensive contextual coverage for each candidate. This technique is inspired by self-reflection methods \[<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">14</a>\], which iteratively refine generated responses by learning from prior outputs. In <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>, we adapt this approach to the specific needs of context-complete vulnerability analysis.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Given a security test case function $f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t}$, a vulnerable API $A\hspace{0pt}P\hspace{0pt}I_{v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l}$, and an initial candidate set $C_{c\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}e} = {\{{c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{n}}\}}$, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> iteratively expands the context through the following steps:

- <span id="S4.I2.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I2.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I2.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Reflection Query:</span> For each candidate $c_{i} \in C_{c\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}e}$, <span id="S4.I2.i1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> querys  
  $Q{(c_{i},{A\hspace{0pt}P\hspace{0pt}I_{v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l}},f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t})}$ the LLM: $Q{(c_{i},{A\hspace{0pt}P\hspace{0pt}I_{v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l}},f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t})}$  
  $\phantom{}\rightarrow{(\left. \{{Y\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s} \middle| {N\hspace{0pt}o}\} \right.,\text{reason})}$. “Yes" indicates sufficient context for vulnerability assessment, while “No" is accompanied by a reason explaining the missing context. This feedback guides subsequent steps.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I2.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I2.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I2.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Code Inference:</span> If ${Q{(c_{i},{A\hspace{0pt}P\hspace{0pt}I_{v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l}},f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t})}}\rightarrow{({N\hspace{0pt}o},\text{reason})}$, <span id="S4.I2.i2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> generates a code inference query $P{(c_{i},{A\hspace{0pt}P\hspace{0pt}I_{v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l}},f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t},\text{reason})}$ to the LLM: ${P{(c_{i},{A\hspace{0pt}P\hspace{0pt}I_{v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l}},f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t},\text{reason})}}\rightarrow{(c_{m\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}g},{s\hspace{0pt}c\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}e})}$. Here, $c_{m\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}g}$ represents the inferred code snippet to search for, while $s\hspace{0pt}c\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}e$ defines required metadata constraints, like class, method, and filepath, ensuring retrieved code meets both functional similarity and structural specifications.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I2.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I2.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I2.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Iterative Context Expansion via Code Retrieval:</span> While the initial candidate identification in Section <a href="#S4.SS3.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="4.3.1 Candidate identification ‣ 4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.3.1</span></a> uses only code similarity, here <span id="S4.I2.i3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> conducts retrieval using both $c_{m\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}g}$ for embedding similarity search and $s\hspace{0pt}c\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}p\hspace{0pt}e$ to filter results based on structural constraints. This enhanced retrieval process yields a new set of candidates $C_{m\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}g}$ that are both syntactically similar and structurally relevant. This set is then merged with the existing candidates: $C_{c\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}e}^{\prime} = {C_{c\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}d\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}e} \cup C_{m\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}g}}$.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

These three steps work synergistically to iteratively build a more complete code context. The LLM’s feedback in the Reflection Query drives the Code Inference step, which in turn informs the Context Expansion. This iterative process continues until one of the termination conditions is met:

- <span id="S4.I3.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I3.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  The LLM assesses vulnerability by $Q{(c_{i},{A\hspace{0pt}P\hspace{0pt}I_{v\hspace{0pt}u\hspace{0pt}l}},f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t})}$ and confirms the current context adequacy.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I3.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I3.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  The candidate identification process yields $C_{m\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}i\hspace{0pt}n\hspace{0pt}g} = \varnothing$, confirming no new relevant blocks.

  </div>

</div>

<figure id="alg1" class="ltx_float ltx_float_algorithm ltx_framed ltx_framed_top">
<div id="alg1.6" class="ltx_listing ltx_framed ltx_framed_topbottom">
<div id="alg1.l1" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l1.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">1:</span></span> <span class="math inline"><em>C</em><sub><em>c</em> <em>a</em> <em>n</em> <em>d</em> <em>i</em> <em>d</em> <em>a</em> <em>t</em> <em>e</em> <em>s</em></sub> = {<em>c</em><sub>1</sub>, <em>c</em><sub>2</sub>, …, <em>c</em><sub><em>n</em></sub>}</span><span id="alg1.l1.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">, </span><span class="math inline"><em>A</em> <em>P</em> <em>I</em><sub><em>v</em> <em>u</em> <em>l</em></sub></span><span id="alg1.l1.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">, </span><span class="math inline"><em>f</em><sub><em>t</em> <em>e</em> <em>s</em> <em>t</em></sub></span><span id="alg1.l1.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span>
</div>

<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l2.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">2:</span></span> <span id="alg1.l2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span id="alg1.l2.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">Auxiliary Functions:</span><span id="alg1.l2.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l3" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l3.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">3:</span></span> <span id="alg1.l3.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span id="alg1.l3.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:90%;">Search(code, scope)</span><span id="alg1.l3.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span class="math inline">→</span><span id="alg1.l3.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span class="math inline"><em>C</em></span><span id="alg1.l3.7" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">: Search for similar code and filter by scope constraints. Return a code set </span><span class="math inline"><em>C</em></span><span id="alg1.l3.8" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">. </span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l4" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l4.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">4:</span></span> <span id="alg1.l4.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span id="alg1.l4.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:90%;">Q(<span class="math inline"><em>C</em></span>, <span class="math inline"><em>A</em> <em>P</em> <em>I</em><sub><em>v</em> <em>u</em> <em>l</em></sub></span>, <span class="math inline"><em>f</em><sub><em>t</em> <em>e</em> <em>s</em> <em>t</em></sub></span>)</span><span id="alg1.l4.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span class="math inline">→</span><span id="alg1.l4.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> (boolean, reason): Determines if the code set </span><span class="math inline"><em>C</em></span><span id="alg1.l4.7" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> is semantically complete for </span><span class="math inline"><em>A</em> <em>P</em> <em>I</em><sub><em>v</em> <em>u</em> <em>l</em></sub></span><span id="alg1.l4.8" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> and </span><span class="math inline"><em>f</em><sub><em>t</em> <em>e</em> <em>s</em> <em>t</em></sub></span><span id="alg1.l4.9" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">. </span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l5" class="ltx_listingline">

</div>

</div>
<div id="alg1.l17" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l17.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">17:</span></span> <span id="alg1.l17.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">   </span><span class="math inline"><em>C</em><sub><em>c</em> <em>a</em> <em>n</em> <em>d</em> <em>i</em> <em>d</em> <em>a</em> <em>t</em> <em>e</em> <em>s</em></sub> ← <em>C</em><sub><em>c</em> <em>a</em> <em>n</em> <em>d</em> <em>i</em> <em>d</em> <em>a</em> <em>t</em> <em>e</em> <em>s</em></sub> ∪ <em>C</em><sub><em>c</em> <em>a</em> <em>n</em> <em>d</em> <em>i</em> <em>d</em> <em>a</em> <em>t</em> <em>e</em></sub><sup>′</sup></span><span id="alg1.l17.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l18" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l18.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">18:</span></span> <span id="alg1.l18.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">   </span><span class="math inline">(<em>i</em> <em>s</em> <em>C</em> <em>o</em> <em>m</em> <em>p</em> <em>l</em> <em>e</em> <em>t</em> <em>e</em>, <em>r</em> <em>e</em> <em>a</em> <em>s</em> <em>o</em> <em>n</em>) ← <em>Q</em>(<em>C</em><sup>′</sup>, <em>A</em> <em>P</em> <em>I</em><sub><em>v</em> <em>u</em> <em>l</em></sub>, <em>f</em><sub><em>t</em> <em>e</em> <em>s</em> <em>t</em></sub>)</span><span id="alg1.l18.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l19" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l19.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">19:</span></span> <span id="alg1.l19.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">  </span><span id="alg1.l19.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">end</span><span id="alg1.l19.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span id="alg1.l19.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">while</span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l20" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l20.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">20:</span></span> <span id="alg1.l20.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">end</span><span id="alg1.l20.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> </span><span id="alg1.l20.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">for</span>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_float"><span id="alg1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Algorithm 1</span> </span> Context-Complete Code Reflection-Based Retrieval</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

Algorithm <a href="#alg1" class="ltx_ref" title="Algorithm 1 ‣ 4.3.2 Context-complete Code Retrieval ‣ 4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> provides a detailed outline of this context-complete code retrieval process. The algorithm initializes with a set of candidates and iteratively refines the context for each candidate using the Q (Reflection Query) and P (Code Inference) functions, along with the Search function (from Section <a href="#S4.SS3.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="4.3.1 Candidate identification ‣ 4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.3.1</span></a>). The loop continues until the context is deemed complete or no new candidates are found.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ 4.3.1 Candidate identification ‣ 4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> illustrates <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s iterative context-building process. Starting with an initial code snippet showing a potential null pointer exception in <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">BCryptPasswordEncoder.encode()</span>, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> first identifies a method containing the password encoding implementation. Through reflection analysis, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> determines that this context alone is insufficient to verify the null-check mechanisms for the <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">pwd</span> parameter. When an incomplete context is detected, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> generates targeted queries to retrieve additional code segments, focusing on how <span id="S4.SS3.SSS2.p5.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">pwd</span> is constructed and validated. This iterative retrieval process continues until sufficient context is gathered to perform a comprehensive vulnerability assessment, as indicated by the feedback loop in the figure. The analysis proceeds to the next stage after obtaining complete context.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.3.3 </span>Sematic-Guided Reachability Analysis

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

After obtaining the context-complete candidates, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> performs a semantic-guided reachability analysis to determine if the identified vulnerabilities are exploitable within the target application. This phase leverages an LLM’s ability to understand code semantics and reason about potential exploitability, going beyond traditional static analysis techniques that rely on pattern matching.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

For each context-complete candidate $c_{i}$ in the set $C = {\{ c_{1},{c_{2}\hspace{0pt}\ldots},c_{n}\}}$ <span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> constructs a query for $Q_{i}$ for each candidate $c_{i}$: $Q_{i} = {(c_{i},{A\hspace{0pt}P\hspace{0pt}I},f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t},R,T)}$. Here, $c_{i}$ represents the candidate code snippet, API is the vulnerable API, and $f_{t\hspace{0pt}e\hspace{0pt}s\hspace{0pt}t}$ is the security test case. <span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> instructs the LLM to act as a "Java vulnerability analysis expert" ($R$) and tasks it with determining if the App is affected by the vulnerability ($T$). This query structure provides the LLM with the full context necessary for accurate assessment, including the vulnerable API usage, the security test case, and the contextually relevant code snippets identified during the retrieval phase.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

For instance, in the secure code example discussed in Section <a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref" title="3 Motivation Example ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>, the LLM integrates information from Listing <a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref" title="3 Motivation Example ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> (PoV test) and Figure <a href="#S4.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ 4.3.1 Candidate identification ‣ 4.3 Phase II: Reflection-Based Detection ‣ 4 Design ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> (the context-complete code snippets) into the query $Q_{i}$ to perform the semantic-guided reachability analysis. It evaluates whether the usage of <span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">pwd</span> and <span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">encoder.encode(pwd)</span> could potentially allow setting the null value, as demonstrated in the test function. The LLM provides insights such as: <span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">"The encode method of BCryptPasswordEncoder throws an IllegalArgumentException when a null value is passed as the raw password."</span>. This detailed semantic analysis provides crucial information for both security experts and developers when conducting vulnerability assessments.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

Building on this analysis, <span id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> processes each query $Q_{i}$ independently, returning a binary decision: 1 (vulnerable) or 0 (secure). This independent evaluation allows for potential parallelization, enabling efficient analysis of large-scale projects. The final vulnerability assessment $V\hspace{0pt}D$ for App is determined as follows:

|     |                                                                                                                                            |     |                                                                   |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
|     | $$VD{(App)} = \begin{cases}                                                                                                                
       {{1\hspace{0pt}\text{~(vulnerable)}},} & {{\text{if~}\hspace{0pt}{\exists i}}:{{L\hspace{0pt}L\hspace{0pt}M\hspace{0pt}{(Q_{i})}} = 1}} \\  
       {{0\hspace{0pt}\text{~(secure)}},} & {{\text{if~}\hspace{0pt}{\forall i}}:{{L\hspace{0pt}L\hspace{0pt}M\hspace{0pt}{(Q_{i})}} = 0}}         
       \end{cases}$$                                                                                                                               |     | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(6)</span> |

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p5" class="ltx_para">

The system assigns a vulnerable label when any query $Q_{i}$ indicates potential exploitation while requiring all queries to indicate safety for a secure classification.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS3.p6" class="ltx_para">

While this LLM-powered approach offers significant advantages in understanding code semantics, it is important to acknowledge potential limitations. The accuracy of the assessment depends on the LLM’s ability to comprehend complex code logic and subtle security nuances, and there is a possibility of false positives or negatives.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Evaluation

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

This section presents a comprehensive evaluation of <span id="S5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s ability to identify vulnerabilities arising from library dependencies. We begin by describing the datasets used in our experiments and the metrics used to quantify the effectiveness of vulnerability detection (Section <a href="#S5.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="5.1 Benchmark Dataset And Metrics ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.1</span></a>). Subsequently, we analyze <span id="S5.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s performance, including comparative results against State-Of-The-Art (SOTA) tools (Sections <a href="#S5.SS3" class="ltx_ref" title="5.3 RQ2 (Tool Comparison) ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.3</span></a>–<a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="5.4 RQ3 (Ablation Study) ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.4</span></a>). Finally, we present an ablation study investigating the impact of different model configurations on <span id="S5.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s performance. Our evaluation aims to answer the following Research Questions (RQs):

</div>

<div id="S5.p2" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S5.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ1 (Effectiveness):</span> *How effectively does <span id="S5.I1.i1.p1.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> identify vulnerabilities in real-world projects caused by library dependencies?*

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ2 (Tool Comparison):</span> *How well does <span id="S5.I1.i2.p1.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> compare with SOTA solutions?*

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RQ3 (Ablation Study):</span> *How do different LLMs and embedding models affect <span id="S5.I1.i3.p1.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s ability to detect vulnerable API usage?*

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.1 </span>Benchmark Dataset And Metrics

<div id="S5.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

To evaluate the effectiveness of our tool, we utilized a third-party benchmark \[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>\] comprising 25 distinct vulnerabilities across 55 open-source applications. We list the vulnerable libraries and its corresponding CVEs in Table <a href="#S5.T1" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 1 ‣ 5.1 Benchmark Dataset And Metrics ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>. Within this dataset, 42 applications were identified as vulnerable due to dependencies on vulnerable libraries, while the remaining 13 were confirmed as unaffected. To enhance the accuracy of the ground truth labels, we conducted a manual code audit of all 55 applications. This process involved tracing from the known vulnerable APIs to identify all potentially vulnerable execution paths. The project list and label are listed in Table <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 2 ‣ 5.1 Benchmark Dataset And Metrics ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.

</div>
