<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="S5.T3.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:113%;">Table 3</span>: </span><span id="S5.T3.7" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:113%;">Performance Metrics of Different Tools</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Following prior work \[<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">56</a>\], we leverage four metrics to evaluate <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>:

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Precision (P):</span> The ratio of correctly identified vulnerable projects to the total number of projects flagged as vulnerable by the tool.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

|     |                                                                                                                         |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     | $$P = \frac{\text{\# of correctly identified vulnerable projects}}{\text{Total \# of projects flagged as vulnerable}}$$ |     |

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Recall (R):</span> The ratio of correctly identified vulnerable projects to the total number of actual vulnerable projects in the ground truth dataset.

|     |                                                                                                                              |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     | $$R = \frac{\text{\# of correctly identified vulnerable projects}}{\text{Total \# of vulnerable projects in ground truth}}$$ |     |

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p6" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Accuracy (A):</span> The ratio of correctly classified projects (both vulnerable and non-vulnerable) to the total number of projects in the ground truth dataset.

|     |                                                                                                        |     |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     | $$A = \frac{\text{\# of correctly classified projects}}{\text{Total \# of projects in ground truth}}$$ |     |

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p7" class="ltx_para">

*F-score (F)* is the harmonic mean of precision and recall; it reflects the trade-off between precision and recall.

|     |                                           |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------------|-----|
|     | $$F = \frac{2 \times P \times R}{P + R}$$ |     |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.2 </span>RQ1 (Effectiveness)

<div id="S5.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

To assess the effectiveness of <span id="S5.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> in identifying vulnerabilities stemming from library dependencies in real-world projects (RQ1), we conducted extensive empirical evaluation using a comprehensive benchmark dataset of 55 open-source Java projects \[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>\]. Table <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 2 ‣ 5.1 Benchmark Dataset And Metrics ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> presents the vulnerability analysis results for each project. <span id="S5.SS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> demonstrates strong detection capability by successfully identifying vulnerabilities in 31 out of 42 vulnerable projects, achieving a recall of 73.8%. With the default configuration, <span id="S5.SS2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> flagged 6 additional projects as potentially vulnerable, resulting in a precision of 83.8%, indicating its conservative approach to security assessment.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

A representative example of <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s effectiveness is its analysis of the HTTPCLIENT-1803 \[<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">26</a>\] vulnerability in project gobblin \[<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">10</a>\]. <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> precisely detected a security flaw where malformed URL paths without a leading forward slash could override specified host settings in URIBuilder. Specifically, when analyzing code patterns like <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">new URIBuilder("@notexample.com/mypath").setHost("example.com")</span>, <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> identified that the host parameter fails to be properly enforced, potentially enabling unauthorized host redirection. Through project-wide analysis, <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> confirmed that gobblin’s dependency on this vulnerable API could lead to security risks in its network access controls.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> also demonstrates sophisticated security context analysis, as shown in the Apache Kylin project evaluation. When analyzing BCryptPasswordEncoder usage, <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> identified potential null pointer exceptions in the encode method. However, through comprehensive data flow analysis, <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> verified that the project implements null-check mechanisms before password encoding operations, ensuring that null values cannot reach the encoder. This case demonstrates <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s ability to consider project-specific implementation safeguards when assessing potential vulnerabilities.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

Further analysis reveals <span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s adaptive handling of code complexity at the node level. For example, in the findbugs project \[<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">20</a>\], individual classes like <span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">BugTreeModel</span> and methods like <span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter" style="font-size:80%;">branchOperations</span> contain extensive code blocks within single nodes. <span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> automatically manages such dense code concentrations through its hierarchical decomposition mechanism, though the current tree-sitter parsing implementation may produce fragmented statements when processing these exceptionally large single nodes. This observation provides valuable insights for future optimizations of <span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s AST-enhanced code segmentation.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| <span id="S5.SS2.p5.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:227.6pt;"> <span id="S5.SS2.p5.1.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S5.SS2.p5.1.1.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Finding 1 (response to RQ1:)</span> *With the labeled ground truth, <span id="S5.SS2.p5.1.1.1.1.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> identifies the client project that has been impacted by vulnerable API with 83.8% precision, 73.8% recall, and 78.5% F1. <span id="S5.SS2.p5.1.1.1.1.1.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> has the ability to understand the code semantics with the given sufficient context.*</span> </span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.3 </span>RQ2 (Tool Comparison)

<div id="S5.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To assess the performance of SAVANT relative to existing solutions, we compare it against two representative tools from the domain of traditional SCA:

- <span id="S5.I2.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I2.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I2.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">VAScanner</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>\] utilizes call graph analysis to trace the invocation of vulnerable APIs from library dependencies to the application code. By analyzing the call graph, VAScanner determines whether vulnerable APIs are actually reachable and invoked within the application.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I2.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I2.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I2.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Eclipse Steady</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">22</a>\] combines static and dynamic analysis techniques to perform reachability analysis. It traces the execution paths from a known vulnerable API (the root cause) to its potential invocation points within the application, providing a more detailed assessment of vulnerability exploitation.

  </div>

To ensure a fair comparison, we run experiments for all tools on the same machine. The OS is Ubuntu 24.04.1 LTS with Intel Xeon Silver 4214R and 64GB memory. We implement VAScanner based on their published code \[<a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">66</a>\] and fix program issues based on the paper’s description, adhering to their default settings. We run Eclipse Steady version 3.2.5. We set a 150-minute analysis time limit per project for both Eclipse Steady and VAScanner.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S5.T3" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 3 ‣ 5.1 Benchmark Dataset And Metrics ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> presents a comparative analysis of <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>, Eclipse Steady, and VAScanner across key performance metrics. <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> achieved the highest precision, recall, accuracy, and F1-score, indicating a greater ability to identify vulnerabilities caused by library dependencies within the evaluated Java projects. The lower performance of Eclipse Steady and VAScanner is further illustrated in Table <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 2 ‣ 5.1 Benchmark Dataset And Metrics ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>. <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> successfully analyzed all 55 projects, while Eclipse Steady and VAScanner failed to analyze 17 and 35 projects, respectively.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

For VAScanner, its applicability is limited to Maven projects with standard classpath configurations, and it frequently encounters out-of-memory errors when analyzing large codebases. Examination of the source code reveals a reliance on hard-coded paths for classpath resolution within a Maven-specific design. These constraints, combined with insufficient memory management for large-scale analysis, explain its failure to process a significant portion of the dataset.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

Analysis of Eclipse Steady’s results indicates that even when successfully analyzing projects, Eclipse Steady failed to identify vulnerabilities in cases where <span id="S5.SS3.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> succeeded, such as in the *netarchivesuite* and *PLMCodeTemplate* projects. This observation suggests potential limitations in Eclipse Steady’s static analysis engine, particularly in accurately constructing call graphs and performing comprehensive data flow analysis, which may limit its ability to detect all vulnerable API invocations.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p5" class="ltx_para">

Despite its improved performance compared to the baseline tools, <span id="S5.SS3.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> still exhibits false positives and false negatives, highlighting the inherent challenges in automated vulnerability detection. These findings emphasize the need for continued research and development to enhance the precision and recall of automated vulnerability detection techniques.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| <span id="S5.SS3.p6.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:227.6pt;"> <span id="S5.SS3.p6.1.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S5.SS3.p6.1.1.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Finding 3 (response to RQ2:)</span> *<span id="S5.SS3.p6.1.1.1.1.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> outperforms state-of-the-art solutions in the real-world projects benchmark as SOTAs has faced challenges with the memory limitation and the complex dependencies when building the call graph.*</span> </span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.4 </span>RQ3 (Ablation Study)

<div id="S5.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

To evaluate the performance of various LLMs within the <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> framework, we selected five state-of-the-art closed-source models and one open-source model: <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-4o</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref">51</a>\], <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-o1</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref">51</a>\], <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Claude-3.5-Sonnet</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">9</a>\], <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Google-Gemini-2.0-Flash</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">29</a>\] and <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Llama-3.1-405B</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">7</a>\]. We utilized *voyage-code-3*\[<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">8</a>\] and *OpenAI text-embedding-3-small* \[<a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref">49</a>\] as the embedding models. Furthermore, we explored the impact of varying the maximum code segment size ($\theta$) by evaluating performance with values of 500, 1000, 1500, 2000, 2500, and 3000. Figure <a href="#S5.F3" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 3 ‣ 5.4 RQ3 (Ablation Study) ‣ 5 Evaluation ‣ Savant: Vulnerability Detection in Application Dependencies through Semantic-Guided Reachability Analysis"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> presents the comparative results across four key metrics: precision, recall, accuracy, and F1 score.

</div>

<figure id="S5.F3" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F3.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 3</span>: </span><span id="S5.F3.6" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Performance comparison of different LLMs and embedding models across varying maximum code lengths. The legend follows the format "embedding model-LLM model" (e.g., "openAI-4o" indicates OpenAI embedding model with <span id="S5.F3.6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-4o</span>, and "voyage-claude" represents Voyage embedding model with <span id="S5.F3.6.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Claude-3.5-Sonnet</span>). Each subplot shows a different evaluation metric (precision, recall, accuracy, and F1 score). Different colors represent different LLM models, with green bars indicating OpenAI embeddings and purple bars indicating Voyage embeddings.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Impact of LLMs:</span> Among the tested models, <span id="S5.SS4.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-4o</span> and <span id="S5.SS4.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Google-Gemini-2.0-Flash</span> consistently demonstrate superior performance across all metrics. Particularly, <span id="S5.SS4.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-4o</span> achieves highest F1 scores of 0.87 with OpenAI embeddings at 2,500 tokens, while <span id="S5.SS4.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Google-Gemini-2.0-Flash</span> maintains stable performance with F1 scores above 0.85 across various code lengths. <span id="S5.SS4.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Llama-3.1-405B</span> demonstrates competitive precision scores around 0.85 but shows lower recall rates around 0.60, resulting in F1 scores between 0.64 and 0.74. <span id="S5.SS4.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-o1</span> shows relatively lower performance, with F1 scores ranging from 0.72 to 0.83.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Embedding Model Comparison:</span> The choice of embedding model shows notable impact on performance. OpenAI embeddings generally yield marginally better results compared to Voyage embeddings, particularly evident in precision metrics. This advantage is most pronounced when paired with <span id="S5.SS4.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-4o</span> and <span id="S5.SS4.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Google-Gemini-2.0-Flash</span>, where OpenAI embeddings contribute to approximately 2-3% higher precision scores. However, the performance gap between embedding models narrows at larger code segment sizes, suggesting that the choice of embedding model becomes less critical with increased context. For <span id="S5.SS4.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Llama-3.1-405B</span>, both embedding models show similar precision performance, though Voyage embeddings demonstrate slightly better recall rates at smaller code segment sizes.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS4.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Code Length Analysis:</span> The maximum size of the code segment ($\theta$) significantly influences the model performance. We observe that:

- <span id="S5.I3.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I3.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Performance generally improves as $\theta$ increases from 500 to 2,500 tokens, with optimal results typically achieved around 2,000-2,500 tokens.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I3.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I3.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Beyond 2,500 tokens, performance plateaus or slightly decreases, indicating a potential sweet spot for context window size.

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I3.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I3.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Smaller code segments (500-1,000 tokens) show more variance in performance across different model-embedding combinations, suggesting that larger segments provide more stable results.

  </div>

<span id="S5.SS4.p4.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Trade-offs:</span> Our results reveal important trade-offs between model performance and computational efficiency. While larger code segments generally improve accuracy, they also increase processing time and resource requirements. The optimal configuration appears to be using <span id="S5.SS4.p4.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-4o</span> or <span id="S5.SS4.p4.2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Google-Gemini-2.0-Flash</span> with OpenAI embeddings at a code segment size of 2,500 tokens, balancing performance with computational efficiency. While <span id="S5.SS4.p4.2.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Llama-3.1-405B</span> shows promising precision metrics, its lower recall rates suggest it may be better suited for scenarios where precision is prioritized over comprehensive vulnerability detection.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| <span id="S5.SS4.p5.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top" style="width:227.6pt;"> <span id="S5.SS4.p5.1.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S5.SS4.p5.1.1.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Finding 4 (response to RQ3:)</span> *Different LLMs and embedding models show varying performance in <span id="S5.SS4.p5.1.1.1.1.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>. Models achieve F1 scores ranging from 0.64 to 0.87, with <span id="S5.SS4.p5.1.1.1.1.1.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-4o</span> and <span id="S5.SS4.p5.1.1.1.1.1.2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Google-Gemini-2.0-Flash</span> performing consistently better (F1: 0.82-0.87) than <span id="S5.SS4.p5.1.1.1.1.1.2.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Llama-3.1-405B</span> (F1: 0.64-0.74) and <span id="S5.SS4.p5.1.1.1.1.1.2.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">GPT-o1</span> (F1: 0.72-0.83). The highest scores are observed at code segment sizes between 2,000-2,500 tokens, and OpenAI embeddings generally show a 2-3% higher precision compared to Voyage embeddings for most models.*</span> </span> |

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6 </span>Threats to validity

<div id="S6.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S6.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Internal Validity.</span> 1) Parameter Sensitivity: Our approach’s performance may be sensitive to parameters like similarity thresholds and LLM settings. While we set the temperature to 0 for reproducibility, other parameters (e.g., similarity threshold) may still influence results. Future work will include comprehensive sensitivity analyses. 2) Ground Truth Accuracy: The manual labeling data requires significant effort. Our evaluation’s accuracy relies on the correctness of ground truth data. To ensure labeling accuracy, three authors who have industry experience in security domains labeled the data. However, it is important to note that this approach might impact the overall accuracy of the classification. 3) Prompt Template Limitation: We used only fixed prompt templates for the LLM. This could limit the range of vulnerabilities detected and affect the accuracy of our results. 4) Implementation Choices: <span id="S6.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s effectiveness may be influenced by specific implementation choices (e.g., parser, encoder models).

</div>

<div id="S6.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S6.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">External Validity.</span> The generalizability of our findings may be limited by the following factors: 1) Our observations and conclusions are based on the third-party datasets \[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">34</a>\]. While these provide valuable insights, they may not include all possible scenarios or vulnerabilities present in real-world applications. 2) We evaluated our approach only on Java programs, motivated by both Java’s widespread adoption in OSS and the increasing security concerns surrounding Java supply chain attacks in widely used libraries. Our proposed approach is theoretically language-agnostic. However, this Java-focused evaluation limits the generalizability of our findings, as the effectiveness of our approach may vary across languages with different syntax structures, programming paradigms, or vulnerability patterns. 3) Our current investigation relies on exemplar security tests from libraries to demonstrate vulnerability context. This approach, while effective for our study, may not capture all of the vulnerability manifestations in diverse software ecosystems.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">7 </span>Related Work

<div id="S7.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S7.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Vulnerable API Detection</span> Researchers and engineers created tools to detect the invocation of vulnerable APIs or the insecure use of APIs \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>, <a href="#bib.bib61" class="ltx_ref">61</a>, <a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">57</a>, <a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">35</a>, <a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">55</a>, <a href="#bib.bib60" class="ltx_ref">60</a>, <a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref">54</a>, <a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">19</a>, <a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">76</a>, <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a>, <a href="#bib.bib72" class="ltx_ref">72</a>\]. These tools are essential for identifying code that may introduce security risks through flawed API usage patterns, particularly within Java cryptographic libraries. Specifically, tools such as FindSecBugs \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">24</a>\], SonarQube \[<a href="#bib.bib61" class="ltx_ref">61</a>\], Xanitizer \[<a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">57</a>\], CogniCrypt \[<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">35</a>\], CryptoGuard \[<a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">55</a>\], CryptoTutor \[<a href="#bib.bib60" class="ltx_ref">60</a>\], and SEADER \[<a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">76</a>\] utilize static analysis techniques to verify whether Java cryptographic APIs are invoked with secure parameter configurations and correct sequential orders. Fischer et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">25</a>\] and Xu et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib72" class="ltx_ref">72</a>\] trained models on labeled code snippets that demonstrate both secure and insecure cryptographic API usage. These models are then applied to detect potentially insecure API usage patterns in new Java code snippets, learning from examples to identify code that deviates from secure usage patterns.

</div>

<div id="S7.p2" class="ltx_para">

Our approach distinguishes itself from prior work in two key aspects: it generalizes vulnerability detection across diverse API types beyond specific cases like cryptographic APIs, and incorporates broader program context to accurately assess security implications.

</div>

<div id="S7.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S7.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Deep Learning based Software Vulnerability Detection</span> Deep learning-based software vulnerability detector approaches \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>, <a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>, <a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">41</a>, <a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref">79</a>, <a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>, <a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>, <a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>, <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>\] have been proposed to automatically learn vulnerability patterns from various code representations. IVDetect \[<a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>\] utilized RNN-based models to generate code representations from source code and identify vulnerable code patterns. LineVul \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>\] leverages CodeBERT, a pre-trained model for programming language, to generate code vector representations and employs BERT’s self-attention layers to capture long-term dependencies in code sequences. DeepDFA \[<a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">63</a>\] encodes dataflow analysis information into graph neural networks to better capture program semantics for vulnerability detection. These methods often focus on detecting vulnerabilities within individual functions or code snippets rather than considering the dependency vulnerabilities. Differ from these approaches, <span id="S7.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> detects the vulnerable API usage from library dependency. Furthermore, most of existing deep learning approaches primarily target C/C++ vulnerabilities \[<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">27</a>, <a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">38</a>, <a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">41</a>, <a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref">79</a>, <a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">37</a>, <a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">16</a>, <a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">40</a>\], <span id="S7.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> specifically targets vulnerable API usage detection in Java projects.

</div>

<div id="S7.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S7.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LLM for Vulnerability Detection</span> Recent work has explored LLMs’ capabilities in security tasks \[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>, <a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">46</a>, <a href="#bib.bib78" class="ltx_ref">78</a>, <a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">31</a>, <a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">15</a>, <a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">21</a>, <a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">42</a>, <a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">53</a>\]. SecLLMHolmes \[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>\] evaluates the ability of various LLMs to detect security-related bugs under different configurations, concluding that current models is non-deterministic and not robust. Liu <span id="S7.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">et al.<span id="S7.p4.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_upright">\[</span><a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">42</a><span id="S7.p4.1.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_upright">\]</span></span> and Deng <span id="S7.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">et al.<span id="S7.p4.1.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_upright">\[</span><a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">21</a><span id="S7.p4.1.3.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_upright">\]</span></span> investigate LLMs’ potential in vulnerability management and penetration testing processes, respectively. Several studies have also investigated vulnerable code repair \[<a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">53</a>, <a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>, <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">17</a>, <a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">43</a>, <a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref">70</a>, <a href="#bib.bib68" class="ltx_ref">68</a>\] through LLMs. For instance, Pearce et al. \[<a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">53</a>\] examine LLMs in repairing vulnerable code in a zero-shot setting, finding that while models can successfully repair hand-crafted examples, they struggle with complex, real-world cases.

</div>

<div id="S7.p5" class="ltx_para">

These studies employ either a single-step approach, querying the LLM once for a decision, or require human intervention. In contrast, <span id="S7.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> adopts an iterative, reflection-based method where LLMs automatically refine contextual information through multiple distinct roles. While most works focus on local code context \[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">64</a>, <a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">53</a>, <a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">32</a>, <a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">43</a>, <a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref">70</a>, <a href="#bib.bib68" class="ltx_ref">68</a>\], and RLCE \[<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">17</a>\] considers repository-level context, our approach uniquely uses an LLM reflection-based method for efficient context retrieval and precise vulnerability detection in complex programs.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S8" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">8 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S8.p1" class="ltx_para">

We created <span id="S8.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> —a novel framework to assess vulnerable API impacts through semantic preprocessing and reflection-based detection, integrating LLMs for context-aware vulnerability analysis. Compared with existing SCA tools, <span id="S8.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span> offers more precise vulnerability assessment by understanding semantic context in large projects and provides developers with accurate and actionable API impact analysis. Our evaluation demonstrates <span id="S8.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s effectiveness in identifying actual vulnerable API usage with high precision, recall, and accuracy across various codebases and scenarios. In the future, we will enhance <span id="S8.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Savant</span>’s analysis capabilities by implementing heuristic-based code block splitting to identify contextual vulnerability patterns more effectively. We will also extend support to C and Python codebases, enabling comprehensive vulnerability assessment across diverse software supply chains.

</div>

</div>

Our team has already identified <a href="https://github.com/arXiv/html_feedback/issues" class="ltx_ref" target="_blank">the following issues</a>. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for all.

Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a <a href="https://github.com/brucemiller/LaTeXML/wiki/Porting-LaTeX-packages-for-LaTeXML" class="ltx_ref" target="_blank">list of packages that need conversion</a>, and welcome <a href="https://github.com/brucemiller/LaTeXML/issues" class="ltx_ref" target="_blank">developer contributions</a>.
